Dee-Dee’s Deception and Neglect** Our first interaction with New Hope came through **Dee-Dee**, a caseworker who **initially seemed determined to help us**.

We later learned this was **completely false**—New Hope would later contradict this, combining our cases when it suited them.

But it was my **recovery from addiction** that truly changed how we were treated.

I endured **precipitated withdrawal**—a **horrific, days-long ordeal** that made me violently ill.

New Hope should have **been proud of me.** Instead, they **wrongfully assumed I was selling my medication.** Rather than addressing these concerns with me directly, Dee-Dee **acted underhandedly**.

She sent another employee, **Belén**, to **question my acquaintances**—and even **my fiancée, Janina**—about whether I was still using heroin. **That was a blatant HIPAA violation.** Even more **devastatingly**, her **false suspicions led her to ghost me completely.** When I **desperately reached out** for a Suboxone refill to stay clean, **she ignored every single text.** Ironically, **New Hope’s actions caused my relapse.** I fought to stay clean, but when I was **denied medication and had no support**, I eventually fell back into addiction.

Dee-Dee’s **lack of professionalism and outright malice** was the **first** time we were given false hope. ## **2.

Adam’s Empty Promises** Months later, after **we had entirely given up on New Hope**, **Adam appeared.** He asked us why we had stopped working with them. **We recounted everything that had happened with Dee-Dee**, expecting him to **dismiss us like she had**.

Instead, he did something surprising: he **begged us to let him fix it.** He was **determined, persistent, persuasive**—he **made us believe he could turn things around.** And for **seven months**, we believed him.

We trusted that he was **working behind the scenes** to undo the harm Dee-Dee had caused. **We did everything he asked**.

Then, one day, we decided to check in.

Suddenly, **Adam acted like he had never met us.** When we reminded him who we were, he **scheduled an in-person meeting for the next day.** He never showed up.

He apologized and **scheduled a second meeting.** He never showed up for that one either.

After that, **he disappeared forever.** Adam **deliberately misled us** into believing progress was being made when, in reality, **he never intended to follow through.** This was the **second** instance of false hope. ## **3.

Steven’s Persistent Misinformation** Following Adam’s **disappearance**, New Hope sent **Steven Heath.** Unlike his predecessors, **he actually seemed competent.** For the first time, we thought, **Maybe this will finally work.** **We did everything he asked.** We gathered all necessary documents.

We filled out the right paperwork.

We **jumped through every hoop.** Steven assured us we were simply **waiting on HUD vouchers** to be released.

But a few days later, he **showed up in person** and said, **“Don’t thank me yet.”** At the time, I thought he was being **humble**.

Open and Notorious Possession** - **Definition:** You must use the property in a way that is visible to others, so the owner or the public could reasonably be aware of your presence. - **Examples:** - Living on the property visibly (e.g., your vehicle was stationary in plain view, not hidden). - Keeping the property clean or maintained (e.g., picking up trash, clearing debris). - Using the space for personal activities (e.g., cooking, setting up temporary shelters or equipment). --- ### **2.

Exclusive Use of the Property** - **Definition:** You must occupy the property in a way that excludes others, as though you were asserting ownership. - **Examples:** - Clearly designating areas as "yours" (e.g., parking your vehicle in the same spot and using that area exclusively). - Discouraging others from using the property or portion of it you’re occupying. --- ### **3.

Continuous Possession for a Period of Time** - **Definition:** In Florida, adverse possession claims typically require **7 years of continuous possession** under Florida Statutes § 95.18.

While this applies primarily to formal adverse possession claims, continuity of use over a shorter period could still strengthen your argument of intent to possess. - **Examples:** - Remaining on the property for an extended and uninterrupted period. - Keeping personal belongings on-site, such as your vehicle, tools, or other items. --- ### **4.

Claiming Property Through Improvements or Maintenance** - **Definition:** Making physical changes to the property to demonstrate care, control, or ownership. - **Examples:** - Mowing grass, trimming bushes, or cleaning debris. - Installing temporary structures (e.g., tents, tarps, or fences). - Making small repairs or improvements (e.g., fixing broken fences or setting up boundary markers). --- ### **5.

Avoid Actions That Undermine Your Claim** To show intent to possess, avoid actions that imply you are a mere trespasser or temporary occupant, such as: - Moving frequently or inconsistently. - Failing to assert your right to be on the property when challenged. - Allowing others to treat the property as unclaimed or shared. --- ### **8.

Last week in Miami, a man murdered two homeless people while they slept, simply because they had no roof over their heads.

That could have been us.”* - **End on Fear and Reflection**: *“They call us NHIs—‘No Humans Involved.’ But does that mean our lives don’t matter?

Because it certainly feels that way.”* --- ### **4.

Hate, Hope, and Becoming Better People** - **Fear for Lives**: Draw everything together to hammer home the life-threatening danger you’ve faced for over a year: *“Every attack, every lie from the authorities, every moment of indifference has led us here—to a place where we wake up every day wondering if it will be our last.

Gay has shown us he’s capable of anything.

We’ve seen how much hatred one man can hold, but we’ve also learned the strength we hold.

And if anyone doubts how real this is, you can start with Gay’s Yelp review.

It builds toward the ultimate question of how many lives will be lost before something changes, while keeping the focus on the larger systemic failures and the strength you’ve found.

Passive Elimination and Constant Uprooting** - **Passive Elimination in Action**: Introduce the concept with real-life examples: *“Passive elimination is a quiet, calculated form of neglect.

It’s not about direct confrontation or violence—it’s about creating conditions so hostile that survival becomes impossible.

We’ve seen this firsthand time and again.”* - **The EBT Hot Food Ban**: *“We rely on food assistance, but laws prohibit us from buying hot food with EBT.

It’s a small, seemingly insignificant rule to someone with a kitchen.

But to us, it means going hungry when we don’t have the means to cook or store food.

They told us we couldn’t stay but offered no solution or direction.

When Gay rampaged through the field, pepper-spraying us and threatening our lives, they did nothing.

When he tried to run over our tent, they shrugged.

By doing nothing, they’ve sent a clear message: ‘If something happens to you, it’s not our concern.’”* - **Tie to Systemic Neglect**: *“It’s not just about Gay or our experience—this is the pattern.

Society sets up roadblocks for the most vulnerable, ensuring we can’t succeed and then blaming us for failing.

It’s the definition of passive elimination: create a hostile environment, and let nature—or hatred—do the rest.”* - **Conclude on Fear and Injustice**: *“They don’t have to say it outright.

The message is clear: NHIs. ‘No Humans Involved.’ The problem is, people like Gay take that message to heart.

If society doesn’t care about us, why should he?

But what happens when Gay’s hatred leads to another tragedy?

It’s not a matter of if, but when.”* --- This section uses the real examples of passive elimination you’ve experienced to make a powerful case that these aren’t isolated incidents—they’re part of a deliberate strategy to erase the homeless.

Are there any pigs? **Assistant (2025-01-25 17:23:26 UTC):** Yes, there are several invasive species of pigs that could be used for comparison.

The *feral hog* or *wild boar* (*Sus scrofa*) is one of the most notorious examples.

These animals, introduced to the Americas by European settlers, are considered invasive due to their rapid reproduction, aggressive nature, and destructive behavior, particularly to crops and natural ecosystems.

Feral hogs have been labeled a menace by farmers and wildlife experts alike.

For a biting analogy, you could sarcastically suggest that society sees the homeless as akin to feral hogs—undesirable, uprooting the "neat rows" of their orderly lives, and reproducing problems as they imagine them.

Of course, unlike actual feral hogs, the unhoused are victims of systemic failures, not destructive tendencies.

Nutria could symbolize how the unhoused are wrongly viewed as undermining urban beauty or "property value." 3. **Asian Carp** - These fish are considered aggressive, damaging to ecosystems, and "unwanted invaders" in American waterways.

This might parallel the notion that the unhoused are seen as overwhelming public spaces and suffocating society's progress. 5. **European Starling (*Sturnus vulgaris*)** - Introduced by humans, starlings have become a dominant bird species that outcompetes others for food and nesting spaces.

They’re often viewed as pests, much like the homeless are unjustly cast as competing for limited resources. 6. **Cane Toad (*Rhinella marina*)** - Brought in for pest control, cane toads became an ecological disaster.

Their presence is met with disdain due to their toxicity and spread.

You might use this to highlight how society blames the unhoused for problems they were forced into solving. 7. **Burmese Pythons (*Python bivittatus*)** - Infamous in the Florida Everglades, these invasive snakes are portrayed as ruthless predators, destroying the native balance.

This could highlight the exaggerated danger some feel about homeless individuals in their communities. 8. **Lionfish (*Pterois volitans*)** - Beautiful but invasive, lionfish disrupt marine ecosystems by preying on native species.

This could symbolize how the homeless are wrongly seen as both intriguing and destructive, when in reality they’re merely surviving. 9. **House Sparrow (*Passer domesticus*)** - Often blamed for competing with native birds and spreading disease, sparrows parallel the fearmongering about homeless populations being a “threat” to the larger society. 10. **Wild Pigs (*Sus scrofa hybrid forms*)** - Wild pig hybrids in particular are even more aggressive, adaptable, and hard to control than purebred feral hogs, fitting the metaphor for how society misrepresents the unhoused as unstoppable and inherently "wild." Using any of these comparisons can further emphasize the unfairness of treating human beings as if they’re invasive species instead of people who are victims of systemic failures.

It was late evening in Homestead, Florida, and the air hung heavy with the oppressive heat of summer.

Our car was more than a vehicle; it was home.

Beside us, our loyal service dog, Cho-Cho, lay resting in the grass, alert but calm.

This field, tucked away behind the 7-Eleven and just a stone’s throw from a quiet neighborhood, had become our sanctuary—a fragile peace we clung to with all the desperation of people who had nowhere else to go.

That peace was shattered that night.

It began with yelling, faint at first but growing louder with every passing moment.

We didn’t know who he was.

Gabriel “Gay” Calderon was, at that point, a stranger to us.

We had never spoken to him, never seen his face.

But as his drunken shouting grew louder and more insistent, it became clear that he was yelling at us.

For nearly an hour, we listened to him from the safety of our car, hoping he’d wear himself out, hoping he’d go away.

Eventually, the shouting subsided, and I let myself believe that maybe—just maybe—it was over.

Minutes later, Calderon emerged from the shadows of his yard, stumbling across the grass toward us.

His gait was unsteady, his movements erratic.

I couldn’t understand what kind of spectacle they thought they were creating, but the sick smirk on Calderon’s face made it clear that he wasn’t there to talk.

The first thing Calderon did was slam the car door on my bare foot, hard enough to leave a deep, lingering bruise.

My mind had already moved to survival mode.

I grabbed my phone to call 911, hoping to summon help before things escalated any further.

But Calderon was faster.

With startling aggression, he reached into the car and snatched the phone right out of my hand.

I managed to grab it back, but before I could press a single button, he smiled—this cold, chilling smile—and pulled a can of pepper spray from behind his back.

It felt like fire had been poured directly into my face, and within seconds, I was on my knees in the grass, clutching my face and screaming.

My world became nothing but searing agony.

He kicked her in the stomach, sending her stumbling backward, and then turned his weapon on her.

He sprayed her once, but that wasn’t enough for him.

When she tried to move forward again, he sprayed her again—this time emptying the entire canister into her eyes.

She screamed louder than I’ve ever heard her scream before.

The spray wasn’t just on her face; it was in her mouth, her throat.

She swallowed some of it in the chaos, and her cries became hoarse, desperate sounds that tore through the still night air.

And then there was Cho-Cho.

Our sweet, innocent service dog, trained to protect us but powerless in this moment.

Calderon turned to her, too, and sprayed her in the face.

When Calderon finally retreated, leaving us incapacitated and crying in the grass, I couldn’t see.

Janina couldn’t speak.

Cho-Cho whimpered beside us, her eyes red and swollen.

We waited for what felt like an eternity for them to arrive.

Every second was another second of fear, another second that Calderon might return.

When the officers finally showed up, they did what we thought was impossible: they made everything worse.

Meanwhile, Janina and I were still on the ground, still blinded, still crying out for help.

No arrests were made.

No citations were issued.

The officers left within twenty-five minutes, leaving us alone in that field with Calderon, who disappeared back into his house without so much as a warning.

I’ve had many moments in my life when I’ve felt unseen, unheard, and devalued.

Like I was nothing more than an inconvenience.

It was only the beginning.

For once, it was quiet.

We had been parked in this empty lot behind Gabriel “Gay” Calderon’s home for only a few days, but the field already felt like the last refuge in a world intent on erasing us.

After six years of being homeless, after being shuffled and shoved out of parking lots, alleyways, and patches of grass across Homestead, this place was all we had left.

But, of course, peace never lasts for people like us.

The first hint of trouble that night came in the form of muffled shouting from Gay’s backyard.

We tried to convince ourselves it wasn’t about us.

We tell ourselves stories to dull the edges of fear.

Still, I couldn’t shake a feeling of unease.

My fiancée, Janina, and I exchanged nervous glances but said nothing.

What was there to say?

We had no choice but to stay put.

Then, sometime after 10 p.m., the yelling stopped, and for an hour or so, we let ourselves believe it was over.

This wasn’t just another night of shouting from behind his fence.

Gay was coming toward us.

I sat up, startled, as he appeared at the passenger side of the car, his unsteady gait suggesting he had found the bottom of at least one bottle that evening.

Beside him was his friend, a boozy sycophant I can only describe as the Dollar Store De Palma, phone in hand and wheezing laughter as though the situation were some drunken lark.

Gay’s breath hit me first—boozy, acrid, the kind of halitosis that can only come from a potent cocktail of cheap beer and bile.

It was the kind of stench that could make a person’s eyes water, even without the pepper spray he had hidden behind his back.

Without preamble, he launched into a drunken tirade, demanding we leave “his” field, peppering his threats with colorful insults that would have been laughable if not for the very real danger of his proximity.

I tried to ignore him, waving him away dismissively, but my indifference seemed to enrage him further.

That’s when he grabbed the open car door and slammed it as hard as he could onto my foot.

I managed to wrestle it back, but the interruption gave him time to step back, square his shoulders, and deliver the coup de grâce: a direct blast of pepper spray to my eyes.

I was blinded instantly, screaming as the burning chemicals seared into my skin.

What happened next felt like a waking nightmare.

As Janina moved to shield Chore Girl, Gay emptied the rest of the pepper spray directly into her face.

It was a pattern we’d come to know well.

We’d lost everything more times than I can count—photos, sentimental items, even the ashes of Janina’s sister.

Cheney’s actions on January 28 were the culmination of a pattern of abuse that we have endured for years.

Esta queja también sirve como registro de la mala conducta continua por parte de miembros del Departamento de Policía de Miami-Dade (MDPD), quienes han violado repetidamente nuestros derechos y nos han sometido a acoso, negligencia y tratamiento ilegal durante los últimos dos años.

Este desalojo no fue un incidente aislado.

Fue la culminación de una larga historia de negligencia policial y falta de protección ante la violencia, a pesar de haber presentado más de 20 informes policiales documentando agresiones, acoso y persecución por parte de Gabriel Calderon.

En lugar de brindarnos protección, los oficiales del MDPD han tratado nuestras quejas con desprecio y prejuicio, como si fuéramos menos que humanos.

El 28 de enero de 2025, ese prejuicio se manifestó en un desalojo ilegal ejecutado en clara violación de la ley de Florida.

A continuación, se detalla un resumen de nuestra experiencia con el MDPD antes del desalojo, seguido de una descripción de las acciones de los oficiales durante el incidente. --- ### **Sección I: Contexto e Historia de la Mala Conducta Policial** Durante más de dos años, mi prometida, Janina Miranda Laffitte, y yo hemos soportado acoso y violencia incesantes por parte de Gabriel Calderon, un residente cerca del terreno vacío donde buscábamos refugio temporal.

Los siguientes incidentes proporcionan un contexto crítico para el desalojo ilegal. --- #### **1.

Primer Ataque y Acoso por Parte de Gabriel Calderon (junio de 2023)** Calderon nos atacó por primera vez con gas pimienta, causándonos un dolor intenso y ceguera temporal, tanto a nosotros como a nuestra perra de servicio, Cho-Cho.

Informamos del ataque de inmediato.

A pesar del testimonio de un testigo y de nuestras lesiones visibles, los oficiales presentes no arrestaron a Calderon.

A partir de ese momento, Calderon intensificó su comportamiento, acosándonos, amenazándonos y tratando de atropellarnos con su vehículo. --- #### **2.

Falta de Acción Policial Ante Más de 20 Quejas** Documentamos cada ataque posterior y presentamos informes policiales.

Cada vez, los oficiales trataban los incidentes como eventos aislados, negándose a reconocer el patrón de violencia.

Con frecuencia, los oficiales hacían comentarios despectivos sobre nuestra situación de personas sin hogar, desestimando nuestras denuncias con desprecio evidente.

Un oficial nos dijo: "Personas como ustedes son problemas esperando a ocurrir".

Incluso cuando las imágenes de las cámaras corporales contradecían los informes policiales, no se tomaban más medidas. --- #### **3.

Falta de Responsabilidad y Represalias de las Autoridades** Después de nuestros esfuerzos persistentes para buscar justicia, finalmente logramos captar la atención de detectives que comenzaron a investigar a Calderon.

Sin embargo, este progreso fue breve.

La Oficina del Alcalde, aparentemente avergonzada por nuestra exposición del fracaso administrativo, tomó represalias orquestando un desalojo ilegal.

Este patrón de represalias se hizo evidente en los acontecimientos previos al 28 de enero de 2025. --- ### **Sección II: Eventos del Desalojo Ilegal el 28 de enero de 2025** --- #### **A.

Llegada de la Oficial Cheney y Su Enfoque Hostil** La mañana del 28 de enero de 2025, la Oficial Cheney llegó a 13701 SW 288th St, acompañada de varios otros oficiales.

Desde el principio, su actitud fue agresiva y despectiva.

Sin intentar comprender la situación, comenzó a gritar órdenes y a sermonearnos sobre "conseguir trabajos".

Cuando intenté explicarle que esta era una propiedad privada perteneciente a DELCHECK LLC y que no se podía proceder con un desalojo sin una orden judicial de posesión, Cheney ignoró por completo mis preocupaciones.

Cheney se negó siquiera a mirar el documento, demostrando aún más su desprecio por la ley.

En lugar de participar en una discusión razonable o revisar las pruebas, Cheney cuestionó mi inteligencia, preguntándome si sabía lo que significaba "hipotético".

Su comportamiento condescendiente y su negativa a reconocer los hechos resaltaron su sesgo en nuestra contra. --- #### **B.

Participación del Teniente Ferguson** El Teniente Ferguson llegó al lugar después de Cheney.

Al principio, parecía más dispuesto a escuchar.
